=== PROMPT VARIANT ===
r1

=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
There is a road extending east and west, and N persons are on the road.
The road extends infinitely long to the east and west from a point called the origin.
The i-th person (1\leq i\leq N) is initially at a position X_i meters east from the origin.
The persons can move along the road to the east or west.
Specifically, they can perform the following movement any number of times.

- Choose one person. If there is no other person at the destination, move the chosen person 1 meter east or west.

They have Q tasks in total, and the i-th task (1\leq i\leq Q) is as follows.

- The T_i-th person arrives at coordinate G_i.

Find the minimum total number of movements required to complete all Q tasks in order.

Input

The input is given from Standard Input in the following format:
N
X_1 X_2 \ldots X_N
Q
T_1 G_1
T_2 G_2
\vdots
T_Q G_Q

Output

Print the answer.

Constraints


- 1\leq N\leq2\times10^5
- 0\leq X_1 < X_2 < \dotsb < X_N \leq10^8
- 1\leq Q\leq2\times10^5
- 1\leq T_i\leq N\ (1\leq i\leq Q)
- 0\leq G_i\leq10^8\ (1\leq i\leq Q)
- All input values are integers.

Sample Input 1

5
10 20 30 40 50
4
3 45
4 20
1 35
2 60

Sample Output 1

239

An optimal sequence of movements for the persons is as follows (the positions of the persons are not necessarily drawn to scale):

For each task, the persons move as follows.

- The 4th person moves 6 steps east, and the 3rd person moves 15 steps east.
- The 2nd person moves 2 steps west, the 3rd person moves 26 steps west, and the 4th person moves 26 steps west.
- The 4th person moves 18 steps east, the 3rd person moves 18 steps east, the 2nd person moves 18 steps east, and the 1st person moves 25 steps east.
- The 5th person moves 13 steps east, the 4th person moves 24 steps east, the 3rd person moves 24 steps east, and the 2nd person moves 24 steps east.

The total number of movements is 21+54+79+85=239.
You cannot complete all tasks with a total movement count of 238 or less, so print 239.

Sample Input 2

8
0 1 2 3 4 5 6 100000000
6
1 100000000
8 0
1 100000000
8 4
1 100000000
5 21006578

Sample Output 2

4294967297

Note that some persons may need to move to the west of the origin or more than 10^8 meters to the east of it.
Also, note that the answer may exceed 2^{32}.

Sample Input 3

12
1558 3536 3755 3881 4042 4657 5062 7558 7721 8330 8542 9845
8
9 1694
7 3296
12 5299
5 5195
5 5871
1 2491
8 1149
8 2996

Sample Output 3

89644

Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Try to avoid patterns like these:
- spending too long comparing nearby plans before committing to one workable approach;
- returning from code to broad algorithm discussion without a concrete contradiction;
- turning one local doubt into a full restart when the current draft only needs a small patch.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

For i>T: cost = 0 if a_i >= g, else g - a_i.

Let's verify segment tree operations.

Find last index < g in [T+1,N]:
Function find_last_less(node, nl,nr, ql,qr, g): if outside or mn >= g: return -1. Search right child first then left. O(log N).

Range sum query [l,r]: standard O(log N). But for cost we can compute:
- p = find_first_greater(1, T-1, g). If p == -1, left_cost=0. Else left_cost = range_sum(p, T-1) - g*(T-p).
- mid_cost = abs(A_T - g) (point query).
- p2 = find_last_less(T+1, N, g). If p2 == -1, right_cost=0. Else right_cost = g*(p2 - (T+1) +1) - range_sum(T+1, p2). Note if T+1 > N, range empty.

Then update:
- If p != -1: range_assign(p, T-1, g). Because all indices from p to T-1 have A_i > g? Need ensure if p is first >g in [1,T-1], then all i>=p up to T-1 have A_i >= A_p > g (nondecreasing), so yes. - If p2 != -1: range_assign(T+1, p2, g). Because all i from T+1 to p2 have A_i < g? Since p2 last <g in suffix; for i<=p2, A_i <= A_p2 < g (nondecreasing), yes. Set to g.

Before A nondecreasing.

Now implement segment tree.

Let's design segment tree arrays.

N up to 2e5. Use 1-indexed positions. Build initial A_i = X_i - i (i from 1). Wait if use z=y-i. We'll use 1-index. Need confirm sample2 with this matches. Yes.

Segment tree functions:

We can implement recursive with arrays `sumv`, `mn`, `mx`, `lazy`. Since N=2e5, size 4N+5. Values can be negative/positive.

Build:
```
def build(node, l, r):
    if l==r:
        v = X[l-1] - l  # if X list 0-index
        sumv[node]=mn[node]=mx[node]=v
    else:
        mid=(l+r)//2
        build(node*2,l,mid); build(node*2+1,mid+1,r)
        pull(node)
```
Recursion depth ~18, build O(N). Python recursion okay if set recursionlimit high.

Lazy:
```
def apply(node, l, r, val):
    sumv[node] = val * (r-l+1)
    mn[node] = mx[node] = val
    lazy[node] = val
```
`push(node,l,r)`:
```
if lazy[node] is not None:
    mid=(l+r)//2
    apply(node*2,l,mid,lazy[node])
    apply(node*2+1,mid+1,r,lazy[node])
    lazy[node]=None
```
Need use a sentinel for None; values can be None? Values int, None safe. But lazy array of ints; use `lazy = [None]*(4*N)`.

Range assign:
```
def range_assign(node,l,r,ql,qr,val):
    if ql<=l and r<=qr:
        apply(node,l,r,val); return
    push(node,l,r)
    mid=(l+r)//2
    if ql<=mid: range_assign(left,...)
    if qr>mid: range_assign(right,...)
    pull(node)
```
Range sum:
```
def range_sum(node,l,r,ql,qr):
    if ql<=l and r<=qr: return sumv[node]
    push(node,l,r)
    mid=...
    res=0
    if ql<=mid: res += range_sum(left)
    if qr>mid: res += range_sum(right)
    return res
```
Point query:
```
def point_get(node,l,r,idx):
    if l==r: return sumv[node] # or mn
    push(node,l,r)
    mid...
```
Find first greater in range [ql,qr] with threshold g:
We need smallest index i in [ql,qr] such that A_i > g. Since A nondecreasing globally, but we can search using mx. Recursive:
```
def find_first_greater(node,l,r,ql,qr,g):
    if qr < l or r < ql or mx[node] <= g:
        return -1
    if l == r:
        return l
    push(node,l,r)
    mid=(l+r)//2
    # search left first
    res = -1
    if ql <= mid:
        res = find_first_greater(node*2,l,mid,ql,qr,g)
        if res != -1:
            return res
    if qr > mid:
        res = find_first_greater(node*2+1,mid+1,r,ql,qr,g)
    return res
```
But this may visit many nodes if mx>g but no element >g in range? We check mx[node] <= g for fully covered? The condition `mx[node] <= g` only valid if node segment fully within query? If node segment partially overlaps, mx may be >g due to outside range, causing search into nodes with no qualifying element. We need handle partial overlap carefully. Standard approach: if segment outside or (segment fully inside query and mx <= g) return -1. If segment fully inside query and mx > g, descend. If partial, push and search children. We can implement:
```
def find_first_greater(node,l,r,ql,qr,g):
    if qr < l or r < ql:
        return -1
    if ql <= l and r <= qr and mx[node] <= g:
        return -1
    if l == r:
        return l  # since not pruned and in range, must be >g
    push(node,l,r)
    mid=...
    res = -1
    if ql <= mid:
        res = find_first_greater(left,...)
        if res != -1: return res
    if qr > mid:
        res = find_first_greater(right,...)
    return res
```
But if segment fully inside query and mx>g, we descend. If segment partially overlaps, we don't use mx prune? We can still if mx<=g return -1 regardless of overlap? If mx<=g, no element in entire segment >g, so safe. So:
```
if qr < l or r < ql or mx[node] <= g: return -1
if l==r: return l
...
```
This is safe because mx is max over whole segment; if <=g, no element >g anywhere in segment, so no in query. If mx>g but qualifying element may be outside query, we still descend; partial overlap may cause some extra visits but O(log N + number of boundary nodes) maybe O(log N). Good.

Similarly find_last_less:
```
def find_last_less(node,l,r,ql,qr,g):
    if qr < l or r < ql or mn[node] >= g:
        return -1
    if l == r:
        return l
    push...
    # search right first
    if qr > mid:
        res = find_last_less(right,...)
        if res != -1: return res
    if ql <= mid:
        res = find_last_less(left,...)
    return res
```
Need note: If segment partially overlaps and mn>=g, no element <g in segment, safe. If mn<g but qualifying outside query, descend. Fine.

But wait, because A is nondecreasing, we could find first >g using binary search on point values, but segment tree search fine.

Potential issue with lazy propagation and `mx`/`mn` in partial overlap: `push` before descending. Good.

Range sum after lazy: Need push along path. Good.

Now, is the lower bound/update proof fully valid for all tasks? Need ensure tasks are completed "in order", and after each task target at G. We assumed final positions of other persons can be chosen as clipping and cost equals sum distances. Need prove transition cost equals sum |old A_i - new A_i|. Since old and new z arrays nondecreasing, corresponding y_i = z_i + i strictly increasing. The minimum number of movements to transform from old positions y_i^old to new positions y_i^new with same label order is sum |y_i^old - y_i^new| = sum |z_i^old - z_i^new|. Need be certain this is achievable. We argued order-preserving transformations can achieve sum distances. Need prove or at least rely. But for final solution explanation maybe not needed in code, but we need be confident.

Let's rigorously verify the exclusion process cost equals sum of absolute displacements if order preserved. This is a known property: For N labeled particles on a line with at most one per site, if initial and final positions are both strictly increasing with same labels, then the minimum number of adjacent moves into empty sites equals sum_i |a_i - b_i|. Is it always true? Let's try find counterexample.

Consider N=2, initial positions (0,2), final (1,2)? invalid distinct? final (1,2) sum=1+0=1. Can p1 move from0 to1 while p2 stays2? Initial p1=0,p2=2, empty1. Move p1 0->1, cost1. Works.
Initial (0,2), final (0,1) sum=0+1=1. Move p2 2->1, cost1. Works.
Initial (0,2), final (1,3) sum=1+1=2. Move p2 2->3, p1 0->1 cost2. Works.
Initial (0,2), final (-1,1) sum=1+1=2. Move p1 0->-1, p2 2->1 cost2. Works.
Initial (0,2), final (2,4) sum=2+2=4. Move p2 2->3->4, p1 0->1->2 cost4. Works.
Initial (0,1,2), final (0,2,3) sum=0+1+1=2. Move p3 2->3, p2 1->2 cost2. Works.
Initial (0,1,2), final (1,2,3) sum=3. Move p3 2->3, p2 1->2, p1 0->1 cost3. Works.
Initial (0,1,2), final (2,3,4) sum=6. Move p3 2->3->4, p2 1->2->3, p1 0->1->2 cost6. Works.
Initial (0,1,3), final (1,2,4) sum=1+1+1=3. Move p3 3->4, p2 1->2, p1 0->1 cost3. Works.
Initial (0,2,3), final (1,2,4) sum=1+0+1=2. Move p3 3->4, p1 0->1 cost2. Works.
Initial (0,2,4), final (1,3,5) sum=3. Move p3 4->5, p2 2->3, p1 0->1 cost3. Works.
What about initial (0,3), final (1,2) sum=1+1=2. p1 0->1, p2 3->2 cost2. Works.
Initial (0,3), final (2,3) sum=2+0=2. p1 0->1->2, p2 stays3, cost2. Works.
Initial (0,3), final (0,2) sum=0+1=1. p2 3->2 cost1. Works.

Seems true. Proof: Since order preserved, we can move particles one by one. For each particle i, its path from a_i to b_i is within [min(a_i,b_i), max(a_i,b_i)]. If multiple particles move, their paths may overlap but because final order same, we can schedule:
- Particles that move right (b_i > a_i): process in decreasing i (right to left). Moving a right-moving particle to its final position may require passing through positions occupied by particles to its right that are also moving right? But those have already been moved to final positions, which are to the right of this particle's final position? Need check. If process decreasing i, for particle i moving right, all j>i have been placed at b_j. Since b_i < b_j (order), and a_i < a_j. The interval [a_i, b_i] is to the left of b_j? b_i < b_j, so final positions of right particles are to the right of b_i, not blocking. But what about particles j>i that haven't moved? Actually process decreasing i means larger indices first; for i, all j>i already at b_j. Their positions are > b_i, so path to b_i doesn't cross them. Particles j<i (left) are still at a_j < a_i, so path starts after them. Thus path clear? Need also consider empty sites; if path length L, moving step by step, destination each step may be occupied by some particle not yet moved? For particle i moving right from a_i to b_i, positions a_i+1,...,b_i might contain particles j>i? But those have moved to b_j > b_i, so not in path. Particles j<i are at a_j < a_i, so not in path. Thus path empty. So can move. Similarly particles moving left process increasing i. What if a particle's path contains another particle that also moves left but not yet moved? Process increasing i for left-moving: for i, all j<i already at b_j < b_i, so not in path; j>i still at a_j > a_i? Wait if particle i moves left from a_i to b_i, path [b_i, a_i]. Particles j>i are to the right of a_i, so not in path. Particles j<i already moved to b_j < b_i, so not in path. So clear. If a particle moves right and another moves left, their paths might cross? Since order preserved, if i<j, then a_i<a_j and b_i<b_j. Can i move right and j move left? Then b_i > a_i, b_j < a_j. Is it possible b_i > b_j? No, b_i < b_j. Path of i [a_i,b_i], path of j [b_j,a_j]. Since b_i < b_j, intervals may overlap but i's path ends before j's path starts? Actually i's path up to b_i, j's path from b_j down to a_j. Since b_i < b_j, and a_i < a_j. They could overlap if a_i < b_j and b_i > a_j? But b_i < b_j and a_j > a_i. Need check crossing: i moving right, j moving left. Since final order b_i < b_j. At some time, if both move independently, they might collide. But we can schedule all right-moving particles first (decreasing i), then left-moving (increasing i), or vice versa? If we move right-moving particles first, they may move into positions that left-moving particles need to vacate? But left-moving particles are still at original positions; right-moving particle i path [a_i,b_i] might include original position a_j of a left-moving particle j>i? Since j>i, a_j > a_i. Could a_j <= b_i? If j is left-moving, b_j < a_j. But b_i < b_j (final order), so b_i < b_j < a_j. Thus a_j > b_i. So path of i ends before a_j. Thus doesn't include j's original position. Good. If left-moving particle j<i? j<i, a_j < a_i, path of i starts after a_j. So no. Thus right-moving first safe. Now, let's think about the data structure more. Segment tree with range assign and range sum/min/max. We need ensure find_first_greater and find_last_less work correctly with lazy assignment and nondecreasing invariant. Since we don't explicitly use monotonic in segment tree search except for correctness of update ranges. The search functions find by value, not by monotonic; if array not monotonic, first >g in prefix might not be contiguous. But our invariant ensures monotonic, so update ranges valid. Search functions don't rely on monotonic to find first; they use max. Good.

Let's test find_first_greater with partial overlap and lazy. Suppose query [1,T-1], node segment [1,N] partially overlaps. We check `mx[node] <= g`; if false, descend. It may descend into left child where mx>g due to elements outside query? But if left child partially overlaps query, we still descend; eventually it will prune outside. Complexity maybe O(log N) plus some extra. Let's analyze worst-case for find_first_greater on range [1,T-1] with threshold. The search descends from root to the first qualifying index. At each level, it may visit a sibling whose mx<=g (pruned) or partially overlaps. It won't visit many nodes with mx>g but no qualifying in query? Could happen if mx>g due to element just outside query, and query covers only a small part of segment. Example query [1,1], root mx>g due to element at N. find_first_greater(root,1,N,1,1,g): root mx>g, descend left. At each left child, mx may be >g due to element in right part of that child outside query? It will continue until leaf 1; if A_1<=g, at leaf? We check if l==r return l without checking value? Wait if leaf is within query and mx>g? But mx at leaf is A_1. If A_1<=g, then at the parent, the condition `mx[node] <= g` for the child containing leaf would have been true, so we wouldn't descend into it. However, at root, mx>g due to outside query, we descend left. At left child, if its mx<=g, we would prune. But our code checks `mx[node] <= g` at entry; if left child mx<=g, return -1, then search right. So it won't visit leaf with A_1<=g. Good. It may visit O(log N) nodes along boundary. Fine.

But there is a subtle bug: In `find_first_greater`, if segment fully inside query and mx>g, we descend. If segment partially overlaps and mx>g due to outside query, we may descend into both children; but one child might be entirely outside query and pruned by range check. The other child may have mx<=g, pruned. So O(log N). Good.

Similarly find_last_less.

Now, range_sum queries. We can compute left_cost using range_sum(p, T-1). If p==-1, left_cost=0. But what if T-1 < p? p<=T-1 by search. Good.
Right_cost: p2 = find_last_less(T+1,N,g). If p2==-1, 0. Else count = p2 - (T+1) + 1 = p2 - T. Since T+1 <= p2. range_sum(T+1,p2). cost = g*count - sum. Note g can be negative; but for elements in [T+1,p2], A_i < g, so g - A_i positive. If g negative, count positive, sum negative, cost positive. Good.

Mid cost = abs(A_T - g). Need point query. Could maybe use range_sum(T,T). We'll implement point_get.

```
p = find_first_greater(1, T-1, g)

    range_assign(p, T-1, g)
# point assign T
range_assign(T, T, g)
p2 = find_last_less(T+1, N, g)
if p2 != -1:
    range_assign(T+1, p2, g)
```

- update point A_1=g.
This matches: one person can move freely, cost distance. Good.

T=1:
- prefix empty. p=-1.
- mid cost |A1-g|.
- suffix [2,N] find_last_less. Update suffix chmax to g; point A1=g.
Cost formula: sum_{i>1} max(0,g-A_i) + |A1-g|. Good.

T=N:
- prefix [1,N-1] find_first_greater. Update prefix chmin to g; point A_N=g.
- suffix empty.
Cost: sum_{i<N} max(0,A_i-g) + |A_N-g|. Good.

Initial A_i may be equal (when X_i consecutive). Nondecreasing. Segment tree handles.

Now, let's consider if G_i can be less than T? g negative. Values can be negative. Lazy sentinel None okay.

Now, performance of recursive segment tree.

Let's write code carefully.

```
import sys
sys.setrecursionlimit(1_000_000)

def main():
    input = sys.stdin.buffer.readline
    N = int(input())
    X = list(map(int, input().split()))
    Q = int(input())
    size = 4*N + 5
    sumv = [0]*size
    mn = [0]*size
    mx = [0]*size
    lazy = [None]*size

    def build(node, l, r):
        if l == r:
            v = X[l-1] - l
            sumv[node] = v
            mn[node] = v
            mx[node] = v
        else:
            mid = (l+r)//2
            build(node*2, l, mid)
            build(node*2+1, mid+1, r)
            pull(node)
```
Need define pull before build? In Python, nested functions can call functions defined later if at runtime after definition. We'll define pull before build or use local.

```
    def pull(node):
        left = node*2; right=left+1
        sumv[node] = sumv[left] + sumv[right]
        mn[node] = mn[left] if mn[left] < mn[right] else mn[right]
        mx[node] = mx[left] if mx[left] > mx[right] else mx[right]
```

`apply`:
```
    def apply(node, l, r, val):
        sumv[node] = val * (r - l + 1)
        mn[node] = val
        mx[node] = val
        lazy[node] = val
```
`push`:
```
    def push(node, l, r):
        val = lazy[node]
        if val is not None:
            mid = (l+r)//2
            apply(node*2, l, mid, val)
            apply(node*2+1, mid+1, r, val)
            lazy[node] = None
```
Need if l==r, push not called? In range functions, we call push only if l<r? We can call push in non-leaf; if l==r, no children. In range_assign, if full cover apply; else push. Since if not full cover and l<r. In point_get, if l==r return; else push. In find functions, if l==r return; else push. So safe.

Range assign:
```
    def range_assign(node, l, r, ql, qr, val):
        if ql > r or qr < l:
            return
        if ql <= l and r <= qr:
            apply(node,l,r,val)
            return
        push(node,l,r)
        mid=(l+r)//2
        if ql <= mid:
            range_assign(node*2,l,mid,ql,qr,val)
        if qr > mid:
            range_assign(node*2+1,mid+1,r,ql,qr,val)
        pull(node)
```
Could optimize by checking ql<=qr. We'll guard at call.

Range sum:
```
    def range_sum(node,l,r,ql,qr):
        if ql > r or qr < l:
            return 0
        if ql <= l and r <= qr:
            return sumv[node]
        push(node,l,r)
        mid=(l+r)//2
        res=0
        if ql <= mid:
            res += range_sum(node*2,l,mid,ql,qr)
        if qr > mid:
            res += range_sum(node*2+1,mid+1,r,ql,qr)
        return res
```
Point get:
```
    def point_get(node,l,r,idx):
        if l == r:
            return sumv[node]
        push(node,l,r)
        mid=(l+r)//2
        if idx <= mid:
            return point_get(node*2,l,mid,idx)
        else:
            return point_get(node*2+1,mid+1,r,idx)
```
Find first greater:
```
    def find_first_greater(node,l,r,ql,qr,g):
        if ql > r or qr < l or mx[node] <= g:
            return -1
        if l == r:
            return l
        push(node,l,r)
        mid=(l+r)//2
        res = -1
        if ql <= mid:
            res = find_first_greater(node*2,l,mid,ql,qr,g)
            if res != -1:
                return res
        if qr > mid:
            res = find_first_greater(node*2+1,mid+1,r,ql,qr,g)
        return res
```
Wait: At entry, if `mx[node] <= g`, return -1. This is safe even if segment partially overlaps? Yes, because max over entire segment <=g, so no element in query >g. If segment partially overlaps and mx>g due to outside query, we descend. Good.

But what if segment is fully inside query and mx>g, but l==r? Then return l. Since mx>g, value>g. Good.

Find last less:
```
    def find_last_less(node,l,r,ql,qr,g):
        if ql > r or qr < l or mn[node] >= g:
            return -1
        if l == r:
            return l
        push(node,l,r)
        mid=(l+r)//2
        res = -1
        if qr > mid:
            res = find_last_less(node*2+1,mid+1,r,ql,qr,g)
            if res != -1:
                return res
        if ql <= mid:
            res = find_last_less(node*2,l,mid,ql,qr,g)
        return res
```
If segment partially overlaps and mn<g due to outside query, descend. Good.

Potential issue: `mx[node] <= g` for find_first_greater: If g is very large, all -1. If g is small, root mx>g. Fine.

Now, build with X list. Need if N maybe 0? No.

Input Q after X. We'll read tasks line by line.

```
    ans = 0
    for _ in range(Q):
        T, G = map(int, input().split())
        g = G - T
        # left
        if T > 1:
            p = find_first_greater(1,1,N,1,T-1,g)
        else:
            p = -1
        if p != -1:
            left_cost = range_sum(1,1,N,p,T-1) - g * (T - p)
        else:
            left_cost = 0
        at = point_get(1,1,N,T)
        mid_cost = at - g if at >= g else g - at
        if T < N:
            p2 = find_last_less(1,1,N,T+1,N,g)
        else:
            p2 = -1
        if p2 != -1:
            cnt = p2 - T  # p2 - (T+1) + 1
            right_cost = g * cnt - range_sum(1,1,N,T+1,p2)
        else:
            right_cost = 0
        ans += left_cost + mid_cost + right_cost
        # update
        if p != -1:
            range_assign(1,1,N,p,T-1,g)
        range_assign(1,1,N,T,T,g)
        if p2 != -1:
            range_assign(1,1,N,T+1,p2,g)
    print(ans)
```

Need be careful with `g * (T - p)` when p is index. Count from p to T-1 inclusive = T-p. Yes. For right cnt = p2 - (T+1) +1 = p2 - T. Yes.

Now, is order-preserving WLOG? The rearrangement inequality with target fixed maybe holds because crossing labels is costly. We can rely.

Let's revisit the proof of optimal order-preserving. There is a known result: In 1D exclusion process with labeled particles, the minimum number of moves to transform configuration a to b is sum_i |a_i - b_i| if and only if the order of labels is the same; otherwise it is strictly larger. Moreover, for any b, if we sort the labels by final positions (i.e., replace b by its order-preserving rearrangement with the same multiset), the movement cost does not increase. But target fixed issue: If we sort all labels, target may move. However, we can split at target: labels with index <T must be left of G in any optimal? Let's prove by exchange:

Consider an optimal final configuration b with b_T=G. Suppose there exist i<T and j>T such that b_i > b_j (an inversion across target? Actually if i<T and b_i > G, then since b_T=G, i is right of target; some j>T might be left of G? Not necessarily). We can apply adjacent swaps in the final assignment to sort labels while keeping T at G? Since T is fixed at G, labels less than T should be placed on positions <G, labels greater than T on positions >G. If a left label is to the right of G, then there must be some position <G occupied by a right label? Let's count. Let L = {1..T-1}, R={T+1..N}. There are T-1 left labels and N-T right labels. In final, consider positions <G. They are occupied by some labels. If a left label is at >G, then the number of left labels at <G is at most T-2. The total positions <G could be any number k. If k < T-1, then some left label must be >G. Right labels could also be >G. There may be no right label <G. Example N=3,T=2,G=15, final p1=20,p2=15,p3=30: left label p1>G, no right label <G. Positions <G count0. So sorting with T fixed would require p1<G, but no right label to swap with? We can choose a different multiset: move p1 to <G and p3 maybe? But final positions of others arbitrary, so we can adjust multiset. The order-preserving clipping cost is not just sorting a fixed multiset; it chooses a different multiset (p1=14,p3=30 maybe) with lower cost? Let's compare: initial p1=0,p2=10,p3=20, task p1=15. Clipping cost21 (p1=15,p2=16,p3=20? Actually order-preserving y1=15,y2=16,y3=20? Wait y2>15, choose y2=16, y3=20, cost p1 15+p2 6=21). Final multiset {15,16,20}. The crossed final {15,9,20} had sum distances16 but infeasible. The optimal multiset is {15,16,20}. So not just sorting a given multiset; we choose multiset optimally.

The clipping formula came from L1 isotonic projection with fixed z_T. We proved that using coordinate-wise clipping because a is nondecreasing. That is rigorous for order-preserving configurations. Need show order-preserving restriction is WLOG. Is it always? Let's search for a counterexample where crossing reduces cost below order-preserving projection.

Consider N=2, initial p1=0,p2=100. Task p1=50. Order-preserving cost: y1=50,y2=51 cost50+49=99. Could crossing final p1=50,p2=49 cost50+51=101. Or p2= -1 cost50+101=151. So order-preserving best.
Task p2=50: order cost p2 50, p1 stays0 cost50. Crossing p2=50,p1=51 cost50+51=101. Best order.

N=3, initial p1=0,p2=10,p3=20. Task p2=15. Order cost5. Crossing? p2 final15, p1/p3 can stay? p1=0,p3=20, p2 moves 10->15 path 11..15 empty, cost5. No crossing needed.
Task p2=5. Order cost5. p2 moves west, p1 at0 blocks? p2 from10 to5, path 9..5, p1 at0, clear, cost5. No crossing.
Task p2=0. Order cost10? y2=0, y1<0 choose y1=-1, cost p1 1 + p2 10 =11. Could crossing final p2=0,p1=1? p2 moves west from10 to0, p1 at0 blocks. Need move p1 east to1 (cost1), p2 to0 (cost10), total11. Same. Could p1 stay? no. So order optimal.
Task p2=-5. Order cost p1 to -6 (6) + p2 15 =21. Crossing final p2=-5,p1=1? p2 path from10 to-5 crosses p1 at0. Need move p1 to1 (1), p2 to-5 (15), total16? Let's simulate: Initial p1=0,p2=10,p3=20. Move p1 east to1 (cost1). Now p1 at1. Move p2 west from10 to -5: path 9,8,...,1 occupied by p1! So p2 cannot pass p1 at1. Need move p1 further east to11? But p3 at20, path 2..11? p2 at10, p1 moving east from1 to11 would be blocked by p2 at10. Could move p2 west? Let's find min for p2=-5. To let p2 move west past p1, p1 must move out of p2's path [ -5,10]. p1 initial0. Options: move p1 to -6 (west) cost6, then p2 to -5 cost15, total21 (order-preserving). Or move p1 to11 (east) cost11, but p2 at10 blocks p1 from reaching11? p1 at0, p2 at10. p1 can move to1..9, but 10 occupied. Could move p2 west to9? Then p1 still blocked at10? To get p1 to11, need p2 move east to11? p2 at10 can move east to11 (empty), then p1 to11? occupied. Move p2 to12, p1 to11, p2 to -5? p2 from12 to -5 path crosses p1 at11, blocked. This is messy. Cost:
```
at = point_get(T)
if at == g:
    continue
if at > g:
    # target moves west; only left side can have A_i > g
    p = find_first_greater(1,T-1,g)
    left_cost = 0
    if p != -1:
        left_cost = range_sum(p,T-1) - g*(T-p)
    ans += left_cost + (at - g)
    if p != -1:
        range_assign(p,T-1,g)
    range_assign(T,T,g)
else: # at < g
    p2 = find_last_less(T+1,N,g)
    right_cost = 0
    if p2 != -1:
        cnt = p2 - T
        right_cost = g*cnt - range_sum(T+1,p2)
    ans += right_cost + (g - at)
    range_assign(T,T,g)
    if p2 != -1:
        range_assign(T+1,p2,g)
```

If the inside leaf itself has value<g, then it is a valid answer, and we should return it. At the leaf, l==r and inside query, mn<g (value<g), so return. Good. So safe.

Similarly find_first_greater with partial overlap at right boundary? Query prefix, outside part to the right. If mx>g due to outside right, and inside part all <=g, search left first. Let's draft code:

```python
import sys

def main():
    input = sys.stdin.buffer.readline
    N = int(input())
    X = list(map(int, input().split()))
    Q = int(input())

    size = 4 * N + 5
    sumv = [0] * size
    mn = [0] * size
    mx = [0] * size
    lazy = [None] * size

    def pull(v):
        lch = v * 2
        rch = lch + 1
        sumv[v] = sumv[lch] + sumv[rch]
        mn[v] = mn[lch] if mn[lch] < mn[rch] else mn[rch]
        mx[v] = mx[lch] if mx[lch] > mx[rch] else mx[rch]

    def apply(v, l, r, val):
        sumv[v] = val * (r - l + 1)
        mn[v] = val
        mx[v] = val
        lazy[v] = val

    def push(v, l, r):
        val = lazy[v]
        if val is not None:
            m = (l + r) // 2
            apply(v * 2, l, m, val)
            apply(v * 2 + 1, m + 1, r, val)
            lazy[v] = None

    def build(v, l, r):
        if l == r:
            val = X[l - 1] - l
            sumv[v] = val
            mn[v] = val
            mx[v] = val
        else:
            m = (l + r) // 2
            build(v * 2, l, m)
            build(v * 2 + 1, m + 1, r)
            pull(v)

    def range_assign(v, l, r, ql, qr, val):
        if ql <= l and r <= qr:
            apply(v, l, r, val)
            return
        push(v, l, r)
        m = (l + r) // 2
        if ql <= m:
            range_assign(v * 2, l, m, ql, qr, val)
        if qr > m:
            range_assign(v * 2 + 1, m + 1, r, ql, qr, val)
        pull(v)

    def range_sum(v, l, r, ql, qr):
        if ql <= l and r <= qr:
            return sumv[v]
        push(v, l, r)
        m = (l + r) // 2
        res = 0
        if ql <= m:
            res += range_sum(v * 2, l, m, ql, qr)
        if qr > m:
            res += range_sum(v * 2 + 1, m + 1, r, ql, qr)
        return res

    def point_get(v, l, r, idx):
        if l == r:
            return sumv[v]
        push(v, l, r)
        m = (l + r) // 2
        if idx <= m:
            return point_get(v * 2, l, m, idx)
        else:
            return point_get(v * 2 + 1, m + 1, r, idx)

    def find_first_greater(v, l, r, ql, qr, g):
        if ql > r or qr < l or mx[v] <= g:
            return -1
        if l == r:
            return l
        push(v, l, r)
        m = (l + r) // 2
        if ql <= m:
            res = find_first_greater(v * 2, l, m, ql, qr, g)
            if res != -1:
                return res
        if qr > m:
            return find_first_greater(v * 2 + 1, m + 1, r, ql, qr, g)
        return -1

    def find_last_less(v, l, r, ql, qr, g):
        if ql > r or qr < l or mn[v] >= g:
            return -1
        if l == r:
            return l
        push(v, l, r)
        m = (l + r) // 2
        if qr > m:
            res = find_last_less(v * 2 + 1, m + 1, r, ql, qr, g)
            if res != -1:
                return res
        if ql <= m:
            return find_last_less(v * 2, l, m, ql, qr, g)
        return -1

    build(1, 1, N)
    ans = 0
    for _ in range(Q):
        T, G = map(int, input().split())
        g = G - T
        at = point_get(1, 1, N, T)
        if at == g:
            continue
        if at > g:
            # move target west; only left side may need to be lowered to g
            p = -1
            if T > 1:
                p = find_first_greater(1, 1, N, 1, T - 1, g)
            if p != -1:
                ans += (range_sum(1, 1, N, p, T - 1) - g * (T - p)) + (at - g)
                range_assign(1, 1, N, p, T - 1, g)
            else:
                ans += at - g
            range_assign(1, 1, N, T, T, g)
        else:
            # move target east; only right side may need to be raised to g
            p2 = -1
            if T < N:
                p2 = find_last_less(1, 1, N, T + 1, N, g)
            if p2 != -1:
                cnt = p2 - T
                ans += (g * cnt - range_sum(1, 1, N, T + 1, p2)) + (g - at)
                range_assign(1, 1, N, T + 1, p2, g)
            else:
                ans += g - at
            range_assign(1, 1, N, T, T, g)

    print(ans)

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
```

```
import sys
def main():
    data = list(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))
    idx=0
    N=data[idx]; idx+=1
    X=data[idx:idx+N]; idx+=N
    Q=data[idx]; idx+=1
    ...
    for _ in range(Q):
        T=data[idx]; G=data[idx+1]; idx+=2
```

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with the "minimum total number of movements required to complete all Q tasks in order" where tasks are completed one by one, and between tasks persons can be moved even if not needed for current task to prepare for future? Our greedy update after each task is optimal for current task and also optimal for future? We assumed after each task we set positions to the order-preserving projection for that task. But is it always optimal to perform the locally optimal projection, or could moving persons extra during a task (beyond the minimum for that task) reduce future cost enough to lower total? This is crucial! The problem asks minimum total over all Q tasks in order. We cannot assume greedy local optimality without proof. We need check: For each task, we choose final positions of all persons after task. We minimized the cost for that task given current positions. But maybe a slightly more expensive final configuration for task i could make future tasks cheaper, reducing total. Is our local greedy update globally optimal? This is important.

In sample, local projection matched optimal sequence. But is there a counterexample where paying extra now helps future? Need analyze.

We have a sequence of tasks. At each step, we need choose new positions. The cost is additive. This is like an online/offline optimal control. The state is positions. We assumed the optimal after each task is the L1 projection onto the constraint set (target at G) from current state. But in dynamic programming, the optimal policy might not be greedy if the state transition cost is a metric and the constraint sets vary. However, if the transition cost is the L1 distance (or minimal movement) and we project onto a convex set in z-space (nondecreasing with one coordinate fixed), then the projection is a "metric projection" onto a convex set. For a sequence of projections onto convex sets in a Hilbert space, greedy projection is not generally globally optimal. But here the cost is L1 and the sets are "translation" maybe? Need be careful.

What if task2 target is person1, not person2? Greedy likely.

Let's try a scenario where paying extra in task1 moves a blocker to a position that helps task2. But our greedy already moves blockers only as needed. Extra movement could place blockers closer to future target, reducing future cost. Is there a case where extra movement now saves more later? In 1D with metric projection, moving extra in the same direction as future movement might be neutral (triangle equality) if future target lies beyond. Example start 0, task1 set [10,20], task2 target 100. Greedy to10 cost10, then to100 cost90 total100. Alternative to20 cost20, then to100 cost80 total100. Equal. So extra movement in direction of future doesn't hurt. If future target is opposite, extra hurts. Thus greedy is optimal in 1D because any extra movement beyond the nearest point is in some direction; if future requires same direction, total same; if opposite, worse. In higher dimensions with monotonic constraints, maybe similar: moving extra beyond the projection in the direction of future can be neutral, not beneficial. So greedy should be globally optimal.

Let's try to prove using the "distance to a convex set" projection is a contraction and satisfies for any z in S and any w:
`d(x, P_S(x)) + d(P_S(x), w) <= d(x, z) + d(z, w)`? Is this true for our projection? Let's test with a simple 2D example where S is a line x=y? Start x=(0,0), S={z | z1+z2=10}? Projection under L1 not unique. Choose P=(5,5), z=(10,0), w=(0,10). d(x,P)=10, d(P,w)=10 total20. d(x,z)=10, d(z,w)=20 total30. True. z=(0,10) symmetric. Maybe true for L1 projection onto any convex set? I recall "projection onto a convex set is firmly non-expansive" in Hilbert spaces, but in L1 not necessarily. However, for "median" / isotonic projection, there is a property of being a "least squares" not.

Let's attempt a counterexample in 2D with L1 and convex set S = {z | z1 >= 0, z2 >= 0, z1+z2 >= 10}? Start x=(0,0). Projection onto S? Closest under L1 maybe (0,10) or (10,0) cost10. Choose P=(0,10). Future w=(10,0). Greedy total d(x,P)=10 + d(P,w)=20 =>30. Alternative z=(10,0) cost10 + d(z,w)=20 =>30. Equal. If S is a halfspace, extra along boundary neutral. If S is a point, no choice. For convex sets, the projection is the point in S that minimizes distance; any other z has extra distance in some direction. Future cost difference is at most the extra distance by triangle inequality: d(z,w) >= d(P,w) - d(P,z). Total alternative >= d(x,P)+? Actually d(x,z) >= d(x,P) (by projection). d(z,w) >= d(P,w) - d(P,z). Need show d(x,z)+d(z,w) >= d(x,P)+d(P,w). Since d(x,z) >= d(x,P), but d(z,w) can be much smaller than d(P,w) if z is closer to w. Example x=0, S=[10,20], P=10, z=20, w=15: d(x,z)-d(x,P)=10, d(P,w)-d(z,w)=5-5=0, true. w=100: extra=10, d(P,w)-d(z,w)=90-80=10, equality. w=12: extra=10, d(P,w)-d(z,w)=2-8=-6. True. This suggests greedy is globally optimal due to the "monotone" nature: moving extra in the direction of future is neutral, moving opposite is worse. The local projection is the "minimal" change; any other feasible final position can be obtained from the projection by moving some coordinates further in a direction, and future costs adjust by at most that extra. So greedy optimal.

Let's consider if the local projection is actually the unique "minimal" configuration in a partial order: For target moving west, greedy sets some prefix values down to g; any other feasible final z with z_T=g must have z_i <=? Wait if target moves west (old A_T > g), for i<T, feasible z_i <= g (because nondecreasing and z_T=g). Greedy sets z_i = min(old A_i, g). This is the greatest feasible z_i? Since old A_i may be >g, min gives g; if old A_i<=g, gives old A_i. It is the coordinate-wise maximum feasible vector? Let's see: For i<T, any feasible z_i <= g. Greedy z_i = min(a_i,g), which is the maximum possible value not exceeding both a_i and g. For i>T, feasible z_i >= g; greedy z_i = max(a_i,g) (but in target west case a_i>g, so greedy z_i=a_i, which is the minimum feasible? Actually feasible z_i>=g, greedy keeps a_i, the closest). Thus greedy is the coordinate-wise closest and also "monotone": it is the least (or greatest) change.

For global optimality, if we ever choose a different feasible z at task1, it differs from greedy by moving some coordinates further in one direction. Future tasks' costs are 1-Lipschitz in z; the extra cost now is at least the possible future saving. This is intuitive.

Now, let's think about if there is a known issue with L1 isotonic projection and "clipping" when a is nondecreasing: We used coordinate-wise clipping. This is correct because the constraint set with fixed z_T and monotonicity is exactly {z_i <= g for i<T, z_T=g, z_i >= g for i>T}? Wait, is that true? For nondecreasing z with z_T=g, for i<T, z_i <= z_T = g. For i>T, z_i >= z_T = g. Conversely, if z_i <= g for i<T, z_T=g, z_i >= g for i>T, and the left part is nondecreasing and right part nondecreasing, then global nondecreasing holds because left max <=g <= right min. So the constraint set is actually separable: left values only need be nondecreasing and <=g; right values nondecreasing and >=g. Since a itself is nondecreasing, the coordinate-wise closest left values are min(a_i,g), and right values max(a_i,g). We don't even need to enforce monotonicity within left/right because min/max of a nondecreasing sequence is nondecreasing. Thus the projection is just clipping. This is rigorous.

Now, let's think about if the coordinate-wise lower bound for left side: For i<T, any feasible z_i <= g. The closest value to a_i in (-∞, g] is min(a_i,g). Since these choices are feasible (nondecreasing), they are optimal. Similarly right. So the local task cost is exactly sum of positive deviations. Good.

Now, let's think about if the global optimality of greedy can be proven by induction using the fact that the state after greedy is the coordinate-wise closest and also the "minimal" in a certain order. Suppose at task k, greedy state z^k. For any feasible sequence, at task k the state w^k must satisfy w^k_T = g. We want show greedy total up to k plus optimal future from z^k <= any alternative. This is not trivial but likely true due to the "monotone" structure. Let's see if we can find a counterexample by brute force mentally with N=2, Q=2. We tested several. Let's systematically analyze N=2.

State z=(z1,z2), z1<=z2. Tasks can fix T=1 or T=2.

Case task1 T=1, g1=a; task2 T=2, g2=b.
Greedy task1: z1=a, z2=max(initial z2, a). Cost = |init_z1-a| + max(0, a-init_z2) (if init_z1? Actually if init_z1 maybe >a? But z1<=z2, if init_z1>a, then init_z2>=init_z1>a, so z2 stays init_z2; cost init_z1-a. If init_z1<a, z2=max(init_z2,a), cost a-init_z1 + max(0,a-init_z2). This is the projection.)
Task2 from greedy to z2=b: if b>greedy_z2, cost b-greedy_z2 (z1 stays a if a<=b? Need if b>greedy_z2>=a, so z1=a<=b, feasible; cost b-greedy_z2). If b<greedy_z2, need z1<=b; if a>b, z1 must decrease to b, cost (greedy_z1-b)+(greedy_z2-b) = (a-b)+(greedy_z2-b). If a<=b, z1 stays a, cost greedy_z2-b.
Could an alternative task1 choose z2 larger than greedy to reduce task2 cost if task2 b large? Suppose init z=(0,0), task1 a=10, task2 b=20. Greedy task1 z=(10,10), cost20. Task2 cost10. Total30. Alternative task1 z=(10,20), cost30, task2 cost0. Total30. Equal. If b=15, greedy total20+5=25, alternative30+0? Wait task2 b=15, alternative z=(10,20), at z2=20>15, z1=10<=15, cost5, total35. Greedy better. If b=100, alternative total30+80=110, greedy20+90=110 equal. So no worse.
If task2 b small, alternative larger z2 worse. Thus greedy optimal.

Case task1 T=2, task2 T=1 symmetric. Seems greedy optimal.

For Q>2, by induction, greedy remains optimal because any extra movement in a task can only help if future targets lie in the same direction, and then it doesn't reduce total. This is akin to the "nearest point" algorithm for isotonic regression with time series? I think accepted.

We can model tasks as fixing one coordinate. The greedy projection is coordinate-wise clipping. Suppose task1 T=2, g=10, start a=(0,0,0). Greedy z=(0,10,10) cost20. Task2 T=3, g=20. Seems greedy globally optimal.

Let's think about a more formal argument: The cost function is the L1 distance. The feasible set for each task is a "box" in the partial order? Actually with z_T fixed and monotonic, the set is convex and has a special property: the projection P_S is "monotone" and "nonexpansive" in L1? If P is nonexpansive, then for any w, d(Px, w) <= d(x,w)? Not. But maybe the greedy algorithm is optimal because the value function for future tasks is 1-Lipschitz and the projection minimizes current cost plus any 1-Lipschitz future cost? In dynamic programming, if future cost function F(z) is 1-Lipschitz with respect to L1, then minimizing current cost + F(next) over next state is achieved by the projection? Let's see: We need choose z in S minimizing d(x,z)+F(z). If F is 1-Lipschitz, then F(z) >= F(P) - d(P,z) (since Lipschitz: |F(z)-F(P)| <= d(z,P)). Also d(x,z) >= d(x,P) (P is projection). So d(x,z)+F(z) >= d(x,P)+F(P) - d(P,z) + (d(x,z)-d(x,P)). Need d(x,z)-d(x,P) >= d(P,z)? Not true generally. But if P is the "closest point" and lies between x and z in the sense d(x,z)=d(x,P)+d(P,z), then equality. Is that true for our projection? For any z in S, does d(x,z) = d(x,P)+d(P,z)? Not always. Example x=(0,0), S={z1=10,z2>=10}, P=(10,10), z=(10,20). d(x,z)=30, d(x,P)=20, d(P,z)=10, equality. z=(20,20) d=40, dP=20, d(P,z)=10? d((10,10),(20,20))=20, total40. Equality. z=(10,10) yes. What about z=(10,15): d=25, dP=20, d(P,z)=5, equality. Seems because P is coordinate-wise min/max and S is a box with one side? For target east, S = {z_i<=g for i<T, z_T=g, z_i>=g for i>T, monotonic}. P = clipping. Any z in S differs from P by moving some coordinates further: left coordinates can be <=P_i (if P_i=g or a_i), right coordinates >=P_i. Since x and P and z are coordinate-wise ordered? Let's check. If x_T<g (target east), P_i = max(x_i,g) for i>T, x_i for i<T? Actually for i<T, P_i=min(x_i,g)=x_i (since x_i<=x_T<g). For i>T, P_i=max(x_i,g). For z in S, z_i<=g for i<T, so z_i <= x_i? Not necessarily, x_i could be less than z_i? Since z_i<=g and x_i<g, z_i could be >x_i. Example x=(0,0,0), T=2,g=10, P=(0,10,10). z=(5,10,10) is in S? z1=5<=10, nondecreasing (5<=10<=10), yes. d(x,z)=5+10+10=25, d(x,P)=20, d(P,z)=5, equality. z=(-5,10,10): d=5+10+10=25, dP=20, d(P,z)=5, equality. z=(0,10,20): d=0+10+20=30, dP=20, d(P,z)=10, equality. Seems d(x,z)=d(x,P)+d(P,z) because for each coordinate, z_i lies on the same side of P_i as x_i? Let's check i<T: x_i<g, P_i=x_i. z_i<=g. If z_i>=x_i, then |x_i-z_i| = |x_i-P_i| + |P_i-z_i| (0 + z_i-x_i). If z_i<x_i, then |x_i-z_i| = x_i-z_i, but |P_i-z_i| = x_i-z_i, and |x_i-P_i|=0, so equality. So per coordinate, d(x,z)=d(x,P)+d(P,z) because P_i is between x_i and z_i? Is P_i always between x_i and z_i? For i<T, P_i=x_i, so trivially between x_i and z_i (one endpoint). For i>T, P_i=max(x_i,g). Since z_i>=g. If x_i>=g, P_i=x_i, between. If x_i<g, P_i=g. z_i>=g, so P_i between x_i and z_i. For T, P_T=g=z_T. Thus yes! For any z in S, P is coordinate-wise between x and z. Therefore d(x,z)=d(x,P)+d(P,z). This is the key! Then for any future w, by triangle inequality d(P,z)+d(z,w) >= d(P,w). So d(x,z)+d(z,w) = d(x,P)+d(P,z)+d(z,w) >= d(x,P)+d(P,w). Thus greedy projection is optimal for one-step lookahead. By induction, greedy is globally optimal! Great. This proof relies on P being between x and z for all z in S, which holds for our clipping projection. Excellent.

Thus local greedy is globally optimal. Good.

Now, let's think about if the true feasible set includes non-order-preserving configurations, and the optimal sequence might use them. We argued any final configuration can be replaced by order-preserving one with no greater movement cost. The projection P is in the order-preserving set and between x and any order-preserving z. For non-order-preserving z, we can first sort it to an order-preserving z' with no greater cost from x? Need ensure z' satisfies target fixed. If not, maybe. But the problem's official solution likely uses the order-preserving invariant because labels never need to cross. We can rely.

Let's try to prove order-preserving WLOG more rigorously for our setting:
Given any sequence of movements, consider the order of persons. If two persons cross, we can modify the sequence to avoid crossing without increasing cost? In exclusion process, crossing requires extra moves. There is a known "uncrossing" argument: Since the cost is sum of absolute displacements plus extra for inversions, and the final positions can be sorted by labels to reduce sum distances and avoid extra. For each task, the target coordinate fixed; sorting all final positions by labels might move target. But we can sort the left side and right side relative to target? Let's examine a final configuration b with b_T=G. If some i<T has b_i > G (left person to right of target), then there is at least one position <G? Not necessarily as noted. But we can adjust the multiset: Move that person to some position <G and move the person occupying that position (if any) to b_i? This is swapping final positions of two labels, one left and one right, to reduce inversions. If no position <G, we can create one by moving the rightmost? Hmm.

Maybe the official solution doesn't need to prove order-preserving WLOG separately because the clipping update is derived from considering "persons cannot pass through each other" and the optimal strategy indeed maintains order. Let's accept.

Let's see if a non-order-preserving final configuration could ever have lower movement cost than the order-preserving projection for a single task. We tried and found no. The lower bound using coordinate-wise clipping for order-preserving set might not apply to non-order-preserving, but the true min likely equals it due to the "parking" nature. Let's attempt to prove for a single task directly:

Suppose target T moves to G. Consider any person i<T (initially left of target). In any valid movement sequence, if person i ends to the right of G (i.e., crosses target's final position), then at some time it must occupy the coordinate G? Not exactly, but to go from left of target to right of G, it must cross the point G. Since target ends at G, the target must vacate G before i crosses, and then return to G, adding at least 2 extra moves? This suggests crossing is never beneficial. Similarly for i>T ending left of G. Therefore in an optimal sequence, no person crosses the target's final position G; left persons end left of G, right persons end right of G. This is exactly order relative to target, but labels among left/right could cross each other. However, crossing among left persons or right persons also unnecessary by rearrangement. Thus order-preserving WLOG. Good.

Now, let's think about if a left person i<T could end right of G while target never leaves G? Impossible because G occupied by target at end; to cross, target must move. Extra cost. So optimal avoids.

```python
import sys
sys.setrecursionlimit(1 << 20)

def main():
    input = sys.stdin.buffer.readline
    N = int(input())
    X = list(map(int, input().split()))
    Q = int(input())

    size = 4 * N + 5
    sumv = [0] * size
    mn = [0] * size
    mx = [0] * size
    lazy = [None] * size

    def pull(v):
        lc = v * 2
        rc = lc + 1
        sumv[v] = sumv[lc] + sumv[rc]
        mn[v] = mn[lc] if mn[lc] < mn[rc] else mn[rc]
        mx[v] = mx[lc] if mx[lc] > mx[rc] else mx[rc]

    def apply(v, l, r, val):
        sumv[v] = val * (r - l + 1)
        mn[v] = val
        mx[v] = val
        lazy[v] = val

    def push(v, l, r):
        val = lazy[v]
        if val is not None:
            m = (l + r) // 2
            apply(v * 2, l, m, val)
            apply(v * 2 + 1, m + 1, r, val)
            lazy[v] = None

    def build(v, l, r):
        if l == r:
            val = X[l - 1] - l
            sumv[v] = val
            mn[v] = val
            mx[v] = val
        else:
            m = (l + r) // 2
            build(v * 2, l, m)
            build(v * 2 + 1, m + 1, r)
            pull(v)

    def range_assign(v, l, r, ql, qr, val):
        if ql <= l and r <= qr:
            apply(v, l, r, val)
            return
        push(v, l, r)
        m = (l + r) // 2
        if ql <= m:
            range_assign(v * 2, l, m, ql, qr, val)
        if qr > m:
            range_assign(v * 2 + 1, m + 1, r, ql, qr, val)
        pull(v)

    def range_sum(v, l, r, ql, qr):
        if ql <= l and r <= qr:
            return sumv[v]
        push(v, l, r)
        m = (l + r) // 2
        res = 0
        if ql <= m:
            res += range_sum(v * 2, l, m, ql, qr)
        if qr > m:
            res += range_sum(v * 2 + 1, m + 1, r, ql, qr)
        return res

    def point_get(v, l, r, idx):
        if l == r:
            return sumv[v]
        push(v, l, r)
        m = (l + r) // 2
        if idx <= m:
            return point_get(v * 2, l, m, idx)
        else:
            return point_get(v * 2 + 1, m + 1, r, idx)

    def find_first_greater(v, l, r, ql, qr, g):
        if ql > r or qr < l or mx[v] <= g:
            return -1
        if l == r:
            return l
        push(v, l, r)
        m = (l + r) // 2
        if ql <= m:
            res = find_first_greater(v * 2, l, m, ql, qr, g)
            if res != -1:
                return res
        if qr > m:
            return find_first_greater(v * 2 + 1, m + 1, r, ql, qr, g)
        return -1

    def find_last_less(v, l, r, ql, qr, g):
        if ql > r or qr < l or mn[v] >= g:
            return -1
        if l == r:
            return l
        push(v, l, r)
        m = (l + r) // 2
        if qr > m:
            res = find_last_less(v * 2 + 1, m + 1, r, ql, qr, g)
            if res != -1:
                return res
        if ql <= m:
            return find_last_less(v * 2, l, m, ql, qr, g)
        return -1

    build(1, 1, N)
    ans = 0
    for _ in range(Q):
        T, G = map(int, input().split())
        g = G - T
        at = point_get(1, 1, N, T)
        if at == g:
            continue
        if at > g:
            p = -1
            if T > 1:
                p = find_first_greater(1, 1, N, 1, T - 1, g)
            if p != -1:
                ans += range_sum(1, 1, N, p, T) - g * (T - p + 1)
                range_assign(1, 1, N, p, T, g)
            else:
                ans += at - g
                range_assign(1, 1, N, T, T, g)
        else:
            p2 = -1
            if T < N:
                p2 = find_last_less(1, 1, N, T + 1, N, g)
            if p2 != -1:
                ans += g * (p2 - T + 1) - range_sum(1, 1, N, T, p2)
                range_assign(1, 1, N, T, p2, g)
            else:
                ans += g - at
                range_assign(1, 1, N, T, T, g)

    print(ans)

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
```

Check `range_assign` no outside check: If ql/qr valid, okay. But what if `ql <= l and r <= qr` false and l==r? Then push called on leaf. Could this happen? Suppose query [2,3], node [1,1]? We wouldn't call range_assign on node [1,1] because at parent [1,2], ql<=m? ql=2<=1 false, so no left call. Node [2,2] full cover. So leaf partial not called. Good.

Check `range_sum` no outside check: similar.
